I'm just always worried about something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about specific things or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that's always there?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of specific things, but they just never stop. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next. Like, I'll worry about whether I'll ever get hired again, or... or if I've somehow completely ruined my reputation with the parents. \n\nThen it jumps to the future, and I start stressing over whether my retirement is just slipping away. It even happens with the really little things. I'll make a small mistake during a story-time activity, and suddenly I'm agonizing over whether that means I shouldn't even be trusted with children anymore. It's just a constant loop of those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you often worried about your own health?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, aside from the muscle aches and the jaw pain I mentioned, I don't really worry about getting sick or having some kind of disease. My health is actually pretty fine. It's just... yeah, it's mostly the work and the future stuff that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of your worries tend to revolve around a single area of your life, like work?\n\nPatient: Well, work is definitely the biggest one right now, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just a single area. It's more like it starts with work or the parents, and then it just spills over. \n\nIf I'm not worrying about my job, I'm worrying about my finances, or my retirement, or just making a fool of myself in general. It feels like my brain just finds whatever is available to stress about, so it really covers a lot of ground, not just one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like, I'll worry about whether I'll ever get hired again, or... or if I've somehow completely ruined my reputation with the parents. \n\nThen it jumps to the future, and I start stressing over whether my retirement is just slipping away. It even happens with the really little things. I'll make a small mistake during a story-time activity, and suddenly I'm agonizing over whether that means I shouldn't even be trusted with children anymore. It's just a constant loop of those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you often worried about your own health?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, aside from the muscle aches and the jaw pain I mentioned, I don't really worry about getting sick or having some kind of disease. My health is actually pretty fine. It's just... yeah, it's mostly the work and the future stuff that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of your worries tend to revolve around a single area of your life, like work?\n\nPatient: Well, work is definitely the biggest one right now, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just a single area. It's more like it starts with work or the parents, and then it just spills over. \n\nIf I'm not worrying about my job, I'm worrying about my finances, or my retirement, or just making a fool of myself in general. It feels like my brain just finds whatever is available to stress about, so it really covers a lot of ground, not just one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel sad or... or depressed. It's more just this constant feeling of being on edge or overwhelmed by all the worrying. But I don't feel down in that way, no.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I mean, I'm trying to get back into teaching, but the anxiety is just getting in the way of everything. I've missed a couple of application deadlines because I just couldn't bring myself to finish them. And when I do get an interview, I just... I stumble through it because my mind is racing. I even stopped volunteering for story-time at the library unless someone else handles all the planning. It's just too much. So, yeah, it's definitely keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes, very difficult. I try to pull myself out of it... I'll tell myself to make a list, or maybe pray, or go out and weed the garden. Just, you know, try to focus on the next practical step. But it doesn't really work. It's like one worry just hands off to another, and before I know it, I've lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, other than stepping back from the planning part of story-time like I mentioned, I don't think I'm actively avoiding anything. I still go out, I run errands, I try to do my normal daily routine... I just worry the entire time I'm doing those things. But I'm not staying home or avoiding specific places or situations, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's like I'm constantly bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by the time evening rolls around, my jaw aches so badly that I can hardly even enjoy playing my guitar anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is the pain in your jaw, shoulders, and neck the main thing that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Well, the physical tension is definitely a big part of it, but... no, it's not just the pain. It's more like my mind is constantly racing, and the physical stuff is just the result of that. I'm just always worried about something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about specific things or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that's always there?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of specific things, but they just never stop. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next. Like, I'll worry about whether I'll ever get hired again, or... or if I've somehow completely ruined my reputation with the parents. \n\nThen it jumps to the future, and I start stressing over whether my retirement is just slipping away. It even happens with the really little things. I'll make a small mistake during a story-time activity, and suddenly I'm agonizing over whether that means I shouldn't even be trusted with children anymore. It's just a constant loop of those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you often worried about your own health?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, aside from the muscle aches and the jaw pain I mentioned, I don't really worry about getting sick or having some kind of disease. My health is actually pretty fine. It's just... yeah, it's mostly the work and the future stuff that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of your worries tend to revolve around a single area of your life, like work?\n\nPatient: Well, work is definitely the biggest one right now, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just a single area. It's more like it starts with work or the parents, and then it just spills over. \n\nIf I'm not worrying about my job, I'm worrying about my finances, or my retirement, or just making a fool of myself in general. It feels like my brain just finds whatever is available to stress about, so it really covers a lot of ground, not just one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel sad or... or depressed. It's more just this constant feeling of being on edge or overwhelmed by all the worrying. But I don't feel down in that way, no.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I mean, I'm trying to get back into teaching, but the anxiety is just getting in the way of everything. I've missed a couple of application deadlines because I just couldn't bring myself to finish them. And when I do get an interview, I just... I stumble through it because my mind is racing. I even stopped volunteering for story-time at the library unless someone else handles all the planning. It's just too much. So, yeah, it's definitely keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes, very difficult. I try to pull myself out of it... I'll tell myself to make a list, or maybe pray, or go out and weed the garden. Just, you know, try to focus on the next practical step. But it doesn't really work. It's like one worry just hands off to another, and before I know it, I've lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: No, not really. But I'm not staying home or avoiding specific places or situations, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience stomach problems, headaches, or other physical complaints when you're worried?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't get headaches or stomachaches or anything like that. I know some people get sick to their stomach when they're stressed, but that doesn't really happen to me. It's mostly just... the thoughts racing and feeling tense, but no actual physical pain or stomach issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries tend to focus on a specific type of problem, such as making mistakes or being judged?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really limited to just one specific thing. I'll start thinking that even a small mistake in a story-time activity means I shouldn't be trusted with children anymore. It just never seems to settle on one single issue, it just jumps around all of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are unwanted and popping into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I don't want to be thinking about these things at all. I try telling myself to, you know, make a list, or pray, or go out and weed the garden... just try to focus on the next practical step. It's mostly just... the thoughts racing and feeling tense, but no actual physical pain or stomach issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries tend to focus on a specific type of problem, such as making mistakes or being judged?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really limited to just one specific thing. My mind just keeps circling from one problem to the next. I'll worry about whether I'll ever get hired again, and then that turns into worrying if I've completely ruined my reputation with the parents. Then I start stressing about whether my retirement is just slipping away. And then, yeah, I do worry about making mistakes, too... I'll start thinking that even a small mistake in a story-time activity means I shouldn't be trusted with children anymore. It just never seems to settle on one single issue, it just jumps around all of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are unwanted and popping into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I don't want to be thinking about these things at all. I try telling myself to, you know, make a list, or pray, or go out and weed the garden... just try to focus on the next practical step. But it doesn't really work. They just keep popping up. One worry just hands off to another, and before I know it, I've lost half the afternoon just sitting there, completely caught up in it. I can't seem to stop them once they start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I mean, it's definitely affecting my life right now. Because I get so caught up in it, I've actually missed application deadlines. And when I do manage to get an interview, I just end up stumbling through the whole thing. I've even stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else handles all the planning for it. So, yeah, the anxiety is basically keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. It's... it's very upsetting. Just feeling like I have no control over my own mind, and watching it completely derail my day... it's exhausting. It just wears me down every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any specific sensations, like a racing heart or sweating, when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't get a racing heart or start sweating or anything like that. I haven't really noticed any physical feelings like you're describing. It's mostly just... well, just the constant thoughts in my head, not really a physical thing for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's like I'm constantly bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by the time evening rolls around, my jaw aches so badly that I can hardly even enjoy playing my guitar anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is the pain in your jaw, shoulders, and neck the main thing that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Well, the physical tension is definitely a big part of it, but... no, it's not just the pain. It's more like my mind is constantly racing, and the physical stuff is just the result of that. I'm just always worried about something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about specific things or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that's always there?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of specific things, but they just never stop. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next. Like, I'll worry about whether I'll ever get hired again, or... or if I've somehow completely ruined my reputation with the parents. \n\nThen it jumps to the future, and I start stressing over whether my retirement is just slipping away. It even happens with the really little things. I'll make a small mistake during a story-time activity, and suddenly I'm agonizing over whether that means I shouldn't even be trusted with children anymore. It's just a constant loop of those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you often worried about your own health?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, aside from the muscle aches and the jaw pain I mentioned, I don't really worry about getting sick or having some kind of disease. My health is actually pretty fine. It's just... yeah, it's mostly the work and the future stuff that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of your worries tend to revolve around a single area of your life, like work?\n\nPatient: Well, work is definitely the biggest one right now, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just a single area. It's more like it starts with work or the parents, and then it just spills over. \n\nIf I'm not worrying about my job, I'm worrying about my finances, or my retirement, or just making a fool of myself in general. It feels like my brain just finds whatever is available to stress about, so it really covers a lot of ground, not just one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel sad or... or depressed. It's more just this constant feeling of being on edge or overwhelmed by all the worrying. But I don't feel down in that way, no.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I mean, I'm trying to get back into teaching, but the anxiety is just getting in the way of everything. I've missed a couple of application deadlines because I just couldn't bring myself to finish them. And when I do get an interview, I just... I stumble through it because my mind is racing. I even stopped volunteering for story-time at the library unless someone else handles all the planning. It's just too much. So, yeah, it's definitely keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes, very difficult. I try to pull myself out of it... I'll tell myself to make a list, or maybe pray, or go out and weed the garden. Just, you know, try to focus on the next practical step. But it doesn't really work. It's like one worry just hands off to another, and before I know it, I've lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, other than stepping back from the planning part of story-time like I mentioned, I don't think I'm actively avoiding anything. I still go out, I run errands, I try to do my normal daily routine... I just worry the entire time I'm doing those things. But I'm not staying home or avoiding specific places or situations, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience stomach problems, headaches, or other physical complaints when you're worried?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't get headaches or stomachaches or anything like that. I know some people get sick to their stomach when they're stressed, but that doesn't really happen to me. It's mostly just... the thoughts racing and feeling tense, but no actual physical pain or stomach issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries tend to focus on a specific type of problem, such as making mistakes or being judged?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really limited to just one specific thing. Then I start stressing about whether my retirement is just slipping away. And then, yeah, I do worry about making mistakes, too... I'll start thinking that even a small mistake in a story-time activity means I shouldn't be trusted with children anymore. It just never seems to settle on one single issue, it just jumps around all of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are unwanted and popping into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I don't want to be thinking about these things at all. I try telling myself to, you know, make a list, or pray, or go out and weed the garden... just try to focus on the next practical step. But it doesn't really work. They just keep popping up. One worry just hands off to another, and before I know it, I've lost half the afternoon just sitting there, completely caught up in it. I can't seem to stop them once they start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I mean, it's definitely affecting my life right now. Because I get so caught up in it, I've actually missed application deadlines. And when I do manage to get an interview, I just end up stumbling through the whole thing. I've even stopped volunteering for story-time unless someone else handles all the planning for it. So, yeah, the anxiety is basically keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. It's more like my mind is constantly racing, and the physical stuff is just the result of that. I'm just always worried about something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about specific things or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that's always there?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of specific things, but they just never stop. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next. Like, I'll worry about whether I'll ever get hired again, or... or if I've somehow completely ruined my reputation with the parents. \n\nThen it jumps to the future, and I start stressing over whether my retirement is just slipping away. It even happens with the really little things. I'll make a small mistake during a story-time activity, and suddenly I'm agonizing over whether that means I shouldn't even be trusted with children anymore. It's just a constant loop of those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you often worried about your own health?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, aside from the muscle aches and the jaw pain I mentioned, I don't really worry about getting sick or having some kind of disease. My health is actually pretty fine. It's just... yeah, it's mostly the work and the future stuff that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of your worries tend to revolve around a single area of your life, like work?\n\nPatient: Well, work is definitely the biggest one right now, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just a single area. It's more like it starts with work or the parents, and then it just spills over. \n\nIf I'm not worrying about my job, I'm worrying about my finances, or my retirement, or just making a fool of myself in general. It feels like my brain just finds whatever is available to stress about, so it really covers a lot of ground, not just one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel sad or... or depressed. It's more just this constant feeling of being on edge or overwhelmed by all the worrying. But I don't feel down in that way, no.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries affecting your ability to do the things you need to do on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is. I mean, I'm trying to get back into teaching, but the anxiety is just getting in the way of everything. I've missed a couple of application deadlines because I just couldn't bring myself to finish them. And when I do get an interview, I just... I stumble through it because my mind is racing. I even stopped volunteering for story-time at the library unless someone else handles all the planning. It's just too much. So, yeah, it's definitely keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes, very difficult. I try to pull myself out of it... I'll tell myself to make a list, or maybe pray, or go out and weed the garden. Just, you know, try to focus on the next practical step. But it doesn't really work. It's like one worry just hands off to another, and before I know it, I've lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because of your worries?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'll make a small mistake during a story-time activity, and suddenly I'm agonizing over whether that means I shouldn't even be trusted with children anymore. It's just a constant loop of those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Are you often worried about your own health?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm trying to get back into teaching, but the anxiety is just getting in the way of everything. I even stopped volunteering for story-time at the library unless someone else handles all the planning. It's just too much. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it is. It's actually keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way. I've just missed application deadlines because I get too caught up in it, and the few times I've actually had interviews, I just stumble right through them. I've even stopped volunteering for story-time... well, unless someone else handles all the planning for it. I just can't bring myself to do it otherwise.
